# **🚀 Professional DevOps Tips**

## `.gitignore`

Purpose:  
👉 Keep repository clean and secure

## `.dockerignore`

Purpose:  
👉 Keep Docker image small and fast

# **🔥 Golden DevOps Mindset**

A professional DevOps Engineer does not only deploy applications.

They also:  
✅ optimize repositories  
✅ reduce image size  
✅ improve CI/CD speed  
✅ protect secrets  
✅ maintain clean infrastructure  
✅ improve team collaboration

These small files are part of real-world DevOps engineering.

# **🚀 Golden Rules to Remember for** `.gitignore` **&** `.dockerignore`

# **1. File Name Only = Ignore Exact File**

.env

Meaning:  
Ignore only `.env`

# **2. Folder Name with** `/` **= Ignore Entire Folder**

node_modules/

Meaning:  
Ignore whole directory and everything inside it.

✅ Always use `/` for folders.

# **3.** `*` **= Wildcard Matching**

\*.log

Meaning:  
Ignore ALL `.log` files.

Examples:

- app.log
- error.log
- server.log

# **4.** `!` **= Exception Rule**

\*.log

!important.log

Meaning:  
Ignore all `.log` files EXCEPT `important.log`

Very important rule.

# **5.** `#` **= Comment**

\# Environment Files

Used for:

- organization
- readability
- professional structure

# **6.** `.` **Means Hidden/System File**

.env

.git

Files starting with `.` are hidden files in Linux.

# **7. No Slash = Match Anywhere**

temp

Matches:

- /temp
- src/temp
- app/temp

Anywhere in project.

# **8. Starting Slash** `/` **= Root Directory Only**

/build

Meaning:  
Ignore only root `build` folder.

NOT:

- src/build
- app/build

# **9. Double Star** `**` **= Match Recursively**

\*\*/temp

Meaning:  
Ignore `temp` folder anywhere recursively.

# **10. Order Matters**

Rules are processed top to bottom.

Example:

\*.log

!important.log

Later rule overrides earlier rule.

# **🔥 Most Important Things to Remember**

## **✅** `.gitignore`

Used by Git.

Purpose:  
👉 Prevent tracking unwanted files.

## **✅** `.dockerignore`

Used by Docker.

Purpose:  
👉 Prevent sending unwanted files during image build.

# **🚀 Golden Memory Trick**

Think like this:

| **Symbol** | **Meaning**         |
|------------|---------------------|
| `#`        | Comment             |
| `*`        | Match anything      |
| `!`        | Exception           |
| `/`        | Folder or root path |
| `.`        | Hidden file         |
| `**`       | Recursive matching  |

# **🔥 Golden DevOps Rules**

## **Always Ignore**

.env

node_modules/

\*.log

dist/

build/

# **Never Push Secrets**

.env

\*.pem

\*.key

# **Never Send Large Files into Docker Build**

node_modules/

.git

# **Organize Files Professionally**

    # Environment
    # Dependencies
    # Logs
    # Build Files
    # IDE Files

# **🚀 Golden Final Rule**

If file is:  
❌ temporary  
❌ generated automatically  
❌ secret  
❌ large  
❌ cache-related  
❌ build-related

👉 It probably belongs in `.gitignore` or `.dockerignore`

**My DEVOPS CheatSheet**

📘 Dockerfile Cheatsheet

# **🧠 1. Dockerfile Flow (IMPORTANT)**

Docker reads Dockerfile **top → bottom**

Typical order:

FROM

WORKDIR

COPY

RUN

ENV

EXPOSE

CMD

# **🏗 2. Simple Dockerfile Structure**

Example:

``` dockerfile
# Base Image
FROM node:18-alpine
# Working directory
WORKDIR /app
# Copy files
```

COPY . .

    # Install dependencies
    RUN npm install
    # App environment variable
    ENV PORT=3000
    # App port
    EXPOSE 3000
    # Start application
    CMD ["node", "app.js"]

# **🔥 3. Dockerfile = Build Recipe**

| **Step** | **What Happens**           |
|----------|----------------------------|
| FROM     | Start container OS/runtime |
| WORKDIR  | Create app folder          |
| COPY     | Copy project files         |
| RUN      | Install packages           |
| ENV      | Set variables              |
| EXPOSE   | Document app port          |
| CMD      | Start app                  |

# **⚙️ 4. Most Important Dockerfile Instructions (80/20)**

| **Instruction** | **Purpose**             | **Easy Meaning**    |
|-----------------|-------------------------|---------------------|
| FROM            | Base image              | Starting OS/runtime |
| WORKDIR         | Working folder          | Like `cd`           |
| COPY            | Copy files              | Host → container    |
| RUN             | Execute command         | Install/setup       |
| ENV             | Environment variables   | Config values       |
| EXPOSE          | Document port           | App uses this port  |
| CMD             | Default startup command | Run app             |
| ENTRYPOINT      | Fixed executable        | Main command        |

# **📦 5. FROM**

## **Purpose**

Starting image for container.

``` dockerfile
FROM ubuntu:22.04
FROM node:18
FROM python:3.12
```

## **🧠 Think Like:**

> “Which operating system/runtime do I need?”

# **📁 6. WORKDIR**

## **Purpose**

Set working directory inside container.

    WORKDIR /app

Equivalent to:

``` bash
mkdir /app
cd /app
```

## **🧠 Think Like:**

> “All next commands run inside this folder”

# **📋 7. COPY**

## **Purpose**

Copy files from local machine → container.

COPY . .

Meaning:

| **Left Side** | **Right Side**   |
|---------------|------------------|
| Local files   | Container folder |

Example:

COPY package.json .

    COPY src/ /app/src/

# **🚀 8. RUN**

## **Purpose**

Run commands during image build.

    RUN npm install
    RUN apt update && apt install -y curl

## **Important**

✔ Executes while building image  
✔ Creates new Docker layer

# **🌍 9. ENV**

## **Purpose**

Set environment variables.

    ENV PORT=3000
    ENV NODE_ENV=production

Used by application during runtime.

# **🔓 10. EXPOSE**

## **Purpose**

Document application port.

    EXPOSE 3000

⚠ Important:

EXPOSE does NOT publish port.

Actual publishing:

``` bash
docker run -p 3000:3000 myapp
```

# **▶️ 11. CMD**

## **Purpose**

Default startup command.

    CMD ["node", "app.js"]

Container runs:

node app.js

## **Important**

✔ Only ONE CMD works  
✔ Last CMD overrides previous CMD

# **⚔️ 12. ENTRYPOINT**

## **Purpose**

Fixed executable command.

    ENTRYPOINT ["node"]
    CMD ["app.js"]

Result:

node app.js

# **⚔️ 13. CMD vs ENTRYPOINT**

| **Feature**   | **CMD**         | **ENTRYPOINT**   |
|---------------|-----------------|------------------|
| Purpose       | Default command | Fixed executable |
| Easy override | Yes             | Hard             |
| Flexible      | Yes             | Less             |
| Best use      | Simple apps     | CLI tools        |

# **📥 14. ADD vs COPY**

## **COPY (Recommended)**

COPY . .

Simple file copy only.

## **ADD (Advanced)**

    ADD file.tar.gz /app/

Extra features:

✔ Auto extract `.tar
` ✔ Download URLs

⚠ Best Practice:

> Prefer COPY unless ADD features needed

# **🧱 15. ARG**

## **Purpose**

Build-time variable.

    ARG VERSION=1.0

Used during build only.

Example:

``` dockerfile
FROM ubuntu:${VERSION}
```

Build:

docker build --build-arg VERSION=22.04 .

# **👤 16. USER**

## **Purpose**

Run container as non-root user.

    USER node

✔ Better security  
✔ Recommended in production

# **💾 17. VOLUME**

## **Purpose**

Persistent data storage.

    VOLUME /data

Used for:

- Database data
- Uploads
- Logs

# **🏷 18. LABEL**

## **Purpose**

Metadata.

    LABEL version="1.0"
    LABEL maintainer="admin@example.com"

# **❤️ 19. HEALTHCHECK**

## **Purpose**

Check container health.

    HEALTHCHECK CMD curl --fail http://localhost:3000 || exit 1

Docker checks if app is alive.

# **🛑 20. STOPSIGNAL**

## **Purpose**

Signal sent when stopping container.

    STOPSIGNAL SIGTERM

# **🐚 21. SHELL**

## **Purpose**

Change default shell.

    SHELL ["/bin/bash", "-c"]

# **⚠️ 22. ONBUILD (Advanced)**

Runs instruction when another image uses this image.

    ONBUILD COPY . /app

Rarely used.

# **❌ 23. MAINTAINER (Deprecated)**

Old:

MAINTAINER John

Modern:

    LABEL maintainer="John"

# **🧠 24. Dockerfile Build Process (VERY IMPORTANT)**

When building image:

docker build -t myapp .

Docker:

1.  Reads Dockerfile
2.  Executes instructions top → bottom
3.  Creates image layers
4.  Builds final image

# **🧱 25. Docker Layers (IMPORTANT)**

Each instruction creates a layer.

Example:

``` dockerfile
FROM ubuntu
RUN apt update
RUN apt install curl
```

COPY . .

Layers:

| **Layer** | **Instruction** |
|-----------|-----------------|
| 1         | FROM            |
| 2         | RUN apt update  |
| 3         | RUN apt install |
| 4         | COPY            |

# **🚀 26. Docker Cache (IMPORTANT)**

Docker reuses unchanged layers.

Example:

COPY package.json .

    RUN npm install

COPY . .

Why?

Because dependencies install again ONLY if package.json changes.

✔ Faster builds

# **📂 27. Best Dockerfile Structure for Node.js**

``` dockerfile
FROM node:18-alpine
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm install
```

COPY . .

    EXPOSE 3000
    CMD ["node", "app.js"]

# **🐍 28. Python Dockerfile Example**

``` dockerfile
FROM python:3.12
WORKDIR /app
```

COPY requirements.txt .

    RUN pip install -r requirements.txt

COPY . .

    CMD ["python", "app.py"]

# **☕ 29. Java Dockerfile Example**

``` dockerfile
FROM openjdk:17
WORKDIR /app
```

COPY . .

    RUN javac Main.java
    CMD ["java", "Main"]

# **🧹 30. Dockerfile Best Practices**

| **Best Practice**    | **Why**           |
|----------------------|-------------------|
| Use small images     | Faster + secure   |
| Use alpine images    | Lightweight       |
| Prefer COPY over ADD | Simpler           |
| Use .dockerignore    | Reduce size       |
| Use non-root USER    | Security          |
| Combine RUN commands | Fewer layers      |
| Keep images minimal  | Faster deployment |

# **📄 31. .dockerignore (VERY IMPORTANT)**

Like `.gitignore`

Prevents copying unnecessary files.

Example:

node_modules

.git

.env

logs

# **⚡ 32. Dockerfile Common Commands**

## **Build Image**

docker build -t myapp .

## **Run Container**

``` bash
docker run -p 3000:3000 myapp
```

## **View Images**

``` bash
docker images
```

## **View Containers**

``` bash
docker ps
```

# **🔥 33. Beginner Dockerfile Formula**

Most apps follow this pattern:

``` dockerfile
FROM image
WORKDIR folder
COPY files
RUN install dependencies
COPY app code
EXPOSE port
CMD startup command
```

# **🧠 34. Easy Memory Trick**

Remember:

> 🏗 FROM → 📁 WORKDIR → 📋 COPY → 🚀 RUN → 🌍 ENV → 🔓 EXPOSE → ▶ CMD

# **🎯 35. Real Beginner Mental Model**

Think like this:

| **Dockerfile Instruction** | **Real Meaning**    |
|----------------------------|---------------------|
| FROM                       | Choose computer     |
| WORKDIR                    | Open folder         |
| COPY                       | Put files           |
| RUN                        | Install software    |
| ENV                        | Set settings        |
| EXPOSE                     | App listens on port |
| CMD                        | Start app           |

# **🚨 36. Most Common Beginner Mistakes**

| **Mistake**                | **Problem**     |
|----------------------------|-----------------|
| Forget `.` in build        | Build fails     |
| Wrong COPY path            | Files missing   |
| Multiple CMD               | Only last works |
| EXPOSE thinking opens port | It doesn't      |
| Huge image size            | Slow deployment |
| Running as root            | Security risk   |

# **📌 37. Final Dockerfile Golden Rules**

✅ Keep Dockerfile simple  
✅ Use official base images  
✅ Prefer alpine images  
✅ Use COPY instead of ADD  
✅ Use .dockerignore  
✅ One process per container  
✅ Keep images lightweight  
✅ Understand build layers  
✅ Learn caching behavior

# **🎓 38. One-Line Summary**

> Dockerfile = Step-by-step recipe to build your application container image.

# **📘 GitHub Actions Cheatsheet (Simple DevOps Beginner Guide)**

A GitHub Actions workflow is simply:

> 🤖 “Instructions for GitHub servers to automate tasks”

You tell GitHub:

- WHEN to run
- WHICH machine to use
- WHAT commands to execute

That’s the whole idea.

# **🧠 1. The Most Important Mindset**

❌ Wrong Way:

> Memorize full YAML files

✅ Correct Way:

> Understand workflow structure + think step-by-step

Professional DevOps engineers:

- Copy examples
- Modify small parts
- Debug errors
- Read docs
- Reuse patterns

# **🔥 2. Golden GitHub Actions Structure**

Almost every workflow follows this pattern:

    name: Workflow Name
    on:

trigger-event

    jobs:
    job-name:
    runs-on: machine-name
    steps:

\- step 1

\- step 2

\- step 3

# **🧱 3. Workflow Thinking Formula**

Think in this order:

WHEN → WHAT JOB → WHICH MACHINE → WHAT STEPS

Or:

on → jobs → runs-on → steps

# **📂 4. Where Workflow Files Live**

GitHub Actions files are stored inside:

.github/workflows/

Example:

.github/workflows/deploy.yml

# **⚙️ 5. Core Workflow Sections**

| **Section** | **Purpose**      | **Easy Meaning**        |
|-------------|------------------|-------------------------|
| name        | Workflow name    | Display name            |
| on          | Trigger          | When workflow runs      |
| jobs        | Main tasks       | Big automation tasks    |
| runs-on     | Machine          | OS runner               |
| steps       | Actions/commands | Step-by-step work       |
| uses        | Reuse action     | Use existing automation |
| run         | Execute command  | Run Linux commands      |

# **🏷 6. name**

## **Purpose**

Workflow display name.

name: My First Workflow

Shown inside GitHub Actions tab.

# **🚀 7. on (Trigger)**

## **Purpose**

Defines WHEN workflow runs.

on: push

Meaning:

> Run workflow when code is pushed.

# **🔥 Common Triggers**

| **Trigger**       | **Meaning**        |
|-------------------|--------------------|
| push              | Code pushed        |
| pull_request      | PR created/updated |
| workflow_dispatch | Manual run         |
| schedule          | Cron job           |
| release           | GitHub release     |
| issues            | Issue activity     |

# **🖐 8. Manual Workflow**

    on:
    workflow_dispatch:

Allows:

> “Run workflow manually from GitHub UI”

# **🧱 9. jobs**

## **Purpose**

Main automation tasks.

    jobs:
    build:

Examples:

- Build app
- Run tests
- Deploy app
- Build Docker image

# **🖥 10. runs-on**

## **Purpose**

Which machine GitHub creates.

runs-on: ubuntu-latest

GitHub creates temporary VM/container.

# **💻 Common Runners**

| **Runner**     | **OS**  |
|----------------|---------|
| ubuntu-latest  | Linux   |
| windows-latest | Windows |
| macos-latest   | macOS   |

# **👣 11. steps**

## **Purpose**

Actual step-by-step execution.

steps:

Examples:

- Install packages
- Run tests
- Build app
- Deploy app

# **🔄 12. uses**

## **Purpose**

Reuse existing GitHub Actions.

uses: actions/checkout@v4

Meaning:

> “Use automation already written by others”

# **📥 Most Common Action**

``` yaml
- name: Checkout Code
uses: actions/checkout@v4
```

Purpose:

✔ Downloads repository code into runner

Without checkout:

> Your files are not available.

# **🖥 13. run**

## **Purpose**

Execute terminal commands.

run: npm install

Equivalent to running command in Linux terminal.

# **📦 Multiple Commands**

Use `|`

run: \|

pwd

``` bash
ls
node -v
```

Meaning:

> Run multiple Linux commands.

# **🧠 14. Easy Human Translation Trick**

Whenever confused:

Translate YAML → human language.

Example:

run: npm install

Ask:

> “What command will Linux execute?”

This makes learning MUCH easier.

# **📘 15. Full Beginner Workflow Example**

``` yaml
name: My First Workflow
on:
workflow_dispatch:
jobs:
demo-job:
runs-on: ubuntu-latest
steps:
- name: Print Message
run: echo "Hello DevOps"
- name: Show Current Directory
run: pwd
- name: Show Linux Version
run: uname -a
```

# **🧱 16. How to Read Workflow Mentally**

Example:

    jobs:
    build:

Human meaning:

> “Create a job called build”

Example:

runs-on: ubuntu-latest

Human meaning:

> “Start Ubuntu Linux machine”

Example:

run: npm install

Human meaning:

> “Execute npm install inside Linux”

# **⚠️ 17. YAML Rules (VERY IMPORTANT)**

## **✅ Spaces Matter**

Correct:

    jobs:
    build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest

## **❌ Never Use Tabs**

YAML only likes spaces.

Bad:

    jobs:
    build:

## **✅ Colon** `:` **Means Key → Value**

name: My Workflow

Meaning:

| **Key** | **Value**   |
|---------|-------------|
| name    | My Workflow |

## **✅ Dash** `-` **Means List Item**

``` yaml
steps:
- name: Step 1
- name: Step 2
```

## **✅ Indentation Creates Hierarchy**

    jobs:
    build:

Meaning:

> build belongs inside jobs

# **🧠 18. Easy Memory Formula**

Remember this backbone:

name

on

    jobs

job-name

runs-on

steps

\- name

run

This is the heart of GitHub Actions.

# **🔥 19. Most Common Beginner Workflow**

``` yaml
name: Node App
on:
push:
jobs:
build:
runs-on: ubuntu-latest
steps:
- name: Checkout Code
uses: actions/checkout@v4
- name: Install Dependencies
run: npm install
- name: Run Tests
run: npm test
```

# **🐳 20. Docker Workflow Example**

    name: Docker Build
    on:
    push:
    jobs:
    docker-build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
    - uses: actions/checkout@v4
    - name: Build Docker Image

run: docker build -t myapp .

# **☸️ 21. Kubernetes Deployment Example**

``` yaml
- name: Deploy to Kubernetes
run: kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml
```

# **☁️ 22. AWS Deployment Example**

``` yaml
- name: Deploy to AWS
run: aws s3 sync . s3://my-bucket
```

# **🔐 23. GitHub Secrets**

Store passwords/API keys safely.

GitHub:

Repository → Settings → Secrets

Use inside workflow:

env:

AWS_KEY: \${{ secrets.AWS_KEY }}

# **🌍 24. Environment Variables**

env:

NODE_ENV: production

Accessible in commands.

# **⏱ 25. Scheduled Workflows (Cron)**

Run automatically at time intervals.

    on:
    schedule:
    - cron: "0 0 * * *"

Meaning:

> Run every day at midnight UTC

# **🔀 26. Multiple Jobs**

    jobs:
    build:
    test:
    deploy:

Jobs can run:

- Sequentially
- In parallel

# **🔗 27. Job Dependencies**

    deploy:
    needs: build

Meaning:

> Deploy waits for build job.

# **📂 28. Common DevOps Workflow Pattern**

    Checkout Code
    ↓
    Install Dependencies
    ↓
    Run Tests
    ↓
    Build Application
    ↓
    Build Docker Image
    ↓
    Deploy

# **🧠 29. Real Mental Model**

Think like this:

| **Workflow Part** | **Real Meaning**       |
|-------------------|------------------------|
| on                | When automation starts |
| jobs              | Big tasks              |
| runner            | Temporary VM           |
| steps             | Commands to execute    |
| uses              | Reuse automation       |
| run               | Linux commands         |

# **🚨 30. Common Beginner Mistakes**

| **Mistake**            | **Problem**       |
|------------------------|-------------------|
| Wrong indentation      | YAML fails        |
| Using tabs             | Workflow breaks   |
| Forget checkout action | No code available |
| Wrong runner           | Commands fail     |
| Typo in secrets        | Auth failure      |
| Memorizing YAML        | Confusion         |

# **🔥 31. Professional Learning Method**

Professionals usually:

✅ Read docs  
✅ Copy small examples  
✅ Modify step-by-step  
✅ Debug errors  
✅ Reuse workflows  
✅ Search error messages

Even senior DevOps engineers do this daily.

# **📘 32. Beginner Manual Workflow Example**

``` yaml
name: Beginner Practice
on:
workflow_dispatch:
jobs:
practice-job:
runs-on: ubuntu-latest
steps:
- name: Print Name
run: echo "IT Charlie"
- name: Show Directory
run: pwd
- name: Show Linux Version
run: uname -a
```

# **📌 33. Most Important GitHub Actions Idea**

GitHub Actions is basically:

> Linux automation running in GitHub cloud servers.

If you know:

- Linux commands
- YAML basics
- DevOps flow

Then GitHub Actions becomes MUCH easier.

# **🎯 34. Final Golden Rules**

✅ Understand structure first  
✅ Think step-by-step  
✅ Translate YAML into human language  
✅ Learn Linux commands  
✅ Practice tiny workflows  
✅ Read error logs carefully  
✅ Do NOT memorize giant YAML files  
✅ Copy → modify → learn

# **🧠 35. One-Line Summary**

> GitHub Actions = YAML instructions that tell GitHub WHEN, WHERE, and HOW to automate your DevOps tasks.

# **DEVOPS COMMANDS CHEAT SHEET**

### **Basic Linux Commands**

1.  **pwd** - Print the current working directory.
2.  **ls** - List files and directories.
3.  **cd** - Change directory.
4.  **touch** - Create an empty file.
5.  **mkdir** - Create a new directory.
6.  **rm** - Remove files or directories.
7.  **rmdir** - Remove empty directories.
8.  **cp** - Copy files or directories.
9.  **mv** - Move or rename files and directories.
10. **cat** - Display the content of a file.
11. **echo** - Display a line of text.
12. **clear** - Clear the terminal screen.

### **Intermediate Linux Commands**

13. **chmod** - Change file permissions.
14. **chown** - Change file ownership.
15. **find** - Search for files and directories.
16. **grep** - Search for text in a file.
17. **wc** - Count lines, words, and characters in a file.
18. **head** - Display the first few lines of a file.
19. **tail** - Display the last few lines of a file.
20. **sort** - Sort the contents of a file.
21. **uniq** - Remove duplicate lines from a file.
22. **diff** - Compare two files line by line.
23. **tar** - Archive files into a tarball.
24. **zip/unzip** - Compress and extract ZIP files.
25. **df** - Display disk space usage.
26. **du** - Display directory size.
27. **top** - Monitor system processes in real time.
28. **ps** - Display active processes.
29. **kill** - Terminate a process by its PID.
30. **ping** - Check network connectivity.
31. **wget** - Download files from the internet.
32. **curl** - Transfer data from or to a server.
33. **scp** - Securely copy files between systems.
34. **rsync** - Synchronize files and directories.

### **Advanced Linux Commands**

35. **awk** - Text processing and pattern scanning.
36. **sed** - Stream editor for filtering and transforming text.
37. **cut** - Remove sections from each line of a file.
38. **tr** - Translate or delete characters.
39. **xargs** - Build and execute command lines from standard input.
40. **ln** - Create symbolic or hard links.
41. **df -h** - Display disk usage in human-readable format.
42. **free** - Display memory usage.
43. **iostat** - Display CPU and I/O statistics.
44. **netstat** - Network statistics (use **ss** as modern alternative).
45. **ifconfig/ip** - Configure network interfaces (use **ip** as modern alternative).
46. **iptables** - Configure firewall rules.
47. **systemctl** - Control the systemd system and service manager.
48. **journalctl** - View system logs.
49. **crontab** - Schedule recurring tasks.
50. **at** - Schedule tasks for a specific time.
51. **uptime** - Display system uptime.
52. **whoami** - Display the current user.
53. **users** - List all users currently logged in.
54. **hostname** - Display or set the system hostname.
55. **env** - Display environment variables.
56. **export** - Set environment variables.

### **Networking Commands**

57. **ip addr** - Display or configure IP addresses.
58. **ip route** - Show or manipulate routing tables.
59. **traceroute** - Trace the route packets take to a host.
60. **nslookup** - Query DNS records.
61. **dig** - Query DNS servers.
62. **ssh** - Connect to a remote server via SSH.
63. **ftp** - Transfer files using the FTP protocol.
64. **nmap** - Network scanning and discovery.
65. **telnet** - Communicate with remote hosts.
66. **netcat (nc)** - Read/write data over networks.

### **File Management and Search**

67. **locate** - Find files quickly using a database.
68. **stat** - Display detailed information about a file.
69. **tree** - Display directories as a tree.
70. **file** - Determine a file's type.
71. **basename** - Extract the filename from a path.
72. **dirname** - Extract the directory part of a path.

### **System Monitoring**

73. **vmstat** - Display virtual memory statistics.
74. **htop** - Interactive process viewer (alternative to top).
75. **lsof** - List open files.
76. **dmesg** - Print kernel ring buffer messages.
77. **uptime** - Show how long the system has been running.
78. **iotop** - Display real-time disk I/O by processes.

### **Package Management**

79. **apt** - Package manager for Debian-based distributions.
80. **yum/dnf** - Package manager for RHEL-based distributions.
81. **snap** - Manage snap packages.
82. **rpm** - Manage RPM packages.

### **Disk and Filesystem**

83. **mount/umount** - Mount or unmount filesystems.
84. **fsck** - Check and repair filesystems.
85. **mkfs** - Create a new filesystem.
86. **blkid** - Display information about block devices.
87. **lsblk** - List information about block devices.
88. **parted** - Manage partitions interactively.

### **Scripting and Automation**

89. **bash** - Command interpreter and scripting shell.
90. **sh** - Legacy shell interpreter.
91. **cron** - Automate tasks.
92. **alias** - Create shortcuts for commands.
93. **source** - Execute commands from a file in the current shell.

### **Development and Debugging**

94. **gcc** - Compile C programs.
95. **make** - Build and manage projects.
96. **strace** - Trace system calls and signals.
97. **gdb** - Debug programs.
98. **git** - Version control system.
99. **vim/nano** - Text editors for scripting and editing.

### **Other Useful Commands**

100. **uptime** - Display system uptime.
101. **date** - Display or set the system date and time.
102. **cal** - Display a calendar.
103. **man** - Display the manual for a command.
104. **history** - Show previously executed commands.
105. **alias** - Create custom shortcuts for commands.

### **Basic Git Commands**

1.  **git init** - Initializes a new Git repository in the current directory.
2.  **git clone** - Copies a remote repository to the local machine.
3.  **git status** - Displays the state of the working directory and staging area.
4.  **git add** - Adds changes to the staging area.
5.  **git commit** - Records changes to the repository.
6.  **git config** - Configures user settings such as name and email.
7.  **git log** - Shows the commit history.
8.  **git show** - Displays detailed information about a specific commit.
9.  **git diff** - Shows changes between commits, working directory, and staging area.
10. **git reset** - Unstages changes or resets commits.

### **Branching and Merging**

11. **git branch** - Lists branches or creates a new branch.
12. **git checkout** - Switches between branches or restores files.
13. **git switch** - Switches branches (modern alternative to git checkout).
14. **git merge** - Combines changes from one branch into another.
15. **git rebase** - Moves or combines commits from one branch onto another.
16. **git cherry-pick** - Applies specific commits from one branch to another.

### **Remote Repositories**

17. **git remote** - Manages remote repository connections.
18. **git push** - Sends changes to a remote repository.
19. **git pull** - Fetches and merges changes from a remote repository.
20. **git fetch** - Downloads changes from a remote repository without merging.
21. **git remote -v** - Lists the URLs of remote repositories.

### **Stashing and Cleaning**

22. **git stash** - Temporarily saves changes not yet committed.
23. **git stash pop** - Applies stashed changes and removes them from the stash list.
24. **git stash list** - Lists all stashes.
25. **git clean** - Removes untracked files from the working directory.

### **Tagging**

26. **git tag** - Creates a tag for a specific commit.
27. **git tag -d** - Deletes a tag.
28. **git push --tags** - Pushes tags to a remote repository.

### **Advanced Git Commands**

29. **git bisect** - Finds the commit that introduced a bug.
30. **git blame** - Shows which commit and author modified each line of a file.
31. **git reflog** - Shows a log of changes to the tip of branches.
32. **git submodule** - Manages external repositories as submodules.
33. **git archive** - Creates an archive of the repository files.
34. **git gc** - Cleans up unnecessary files and optimizes the repository.

### **GitHub-Specific Commands**

35. **gh auth login** - Logs into GitHub via the command line.
36. **gh repo clone** - Clones a GitHub repository.
37. **gh issue list** - Lists issues in a GitHub repository.
38. **gh pr create** - Creates a pull request on GitHub.
39. **gh repo create** - Creates a new GitHub repository.

### **Basic Docker Commands**

1.  **docker --version** - Displays the installed Docker version.
2.  **docker info** - Shows system-wide information about Docker.
3.  **docker pull** - Downloads an image from a Docker registry.
4.  **docker images** - Lists all downloaded images.
5.  **docker run** - Creates and starts a new container from an image.
6.  **docker ps** - Lists running containers.
7.  **docker ps -a** - Lists all containers, including stopped ones.
8.  **docker stop** - Stops a running container.
9.  **docker start** - Starts a stopped container.
10. **docker rm** - Removes a container.
11. **docker rmi** - Removes an image.
12. **docker exec** - Runs a command inside a running container.
13. **docker build** - Builds an image from a Dockerfile.
14. **docker commit** - Creates a new image from a container’s changes.
15. **docker logs** - Fetches logs from a container.
16. **docker inspect** - Returns detailed information about a container or image.
17. **docker stats** - Displays live resource usage statistics of running containers.
18. **docker cp** - Copies files between a container and the host.
19. **docker rename** - Renames a container.
20. **docker network ls** - Lists all Docker networks.
21. **docker network create** - Creates a new Docker network.
22. **docker network inspect** - Shows details about a Docker network.
23. **docker network connect** - Connects a container to a network.
24. **docker volume ls** - Lists all Docker volumes.
25. **docker volume create** - Creates a new Docker volume.
26. **docker volume inspect** - Provides details about a volume.
27. **docker volume rm** - Removes a Docker volume.

### **Advanced Docker Commands**

28. **docker-compose up** - Starts services defined in a docker-compose.yml file.
29. **docker-compose down** - Stops and removes services defined in a docker-compose.yml file.
30. **docker-compose logs** - Displays logs for services managed by Docker Compose.
31. **docker-compose exec** - Runs a command in a service’s container.
32. **docker save** - Exports an image to a tar file.
33. **docker load** - Imports an image from a tar file.
34. **docker export** - Exports a container’s filesystem as a tar file.
35. **docker import** - Creates an image from an exported container.
36. **docker system df** - Displays disk usage by Docker objects.
37. **docker system prune** - Cleans up unused Docker resources.
38. **docker tag** - Assigns a new tag to an image.
39. **docker push** - Uploads an image to a Docker registry.
40. **docker login** - Logs into a Docker registry.
41. **docker logout** - Logs out of a Docker registry.
42. **docker swarm init** - Initializes a Docker Swarm mode cluster.
43. **docker service create** - Creates a new service in Swarm mode.
44. **docker stack deploy** - Deploys a stack using a Compose file in Swarm mode.
45. **docker stack rm** - Removes a stack in Swarm mode.
46. **docker checkpoint create** - Creates a checkpoint for a container.
47. **docker checkpoint ls** - Lists checkpoints for a container.
48. **docker checkpoint rm** - Removes a checkpoint.

### **Basic Kubernetes Commands**

1.  **kubectl version** - Displays the Kubernetes client and server version.
2.  **kubectl cluster-info** - Shows information about the Kubernetes cluster.
3.  **kubectl get nodes** - Lists all nodes in the cluster.
4.  **kubectl get pods** - Lists all pods in the default namespace.
5.  **kubectl get services** - Lists all services in the default namespace.
6.  **kubectl get namespaces** - Lists all namespaces in the cluster.
7.  **kubectl describe pod** - Shows detailed information about a specific pod.
8.  **kubectl logs** - Displays logs for a specific pod.
9.  **kubectl create namespace** - Creates a new namespace.
10. **kubectl delete pod** - Deletes a specific pod.

### **Intermediate Kubernetes Commands**

11. **kubectl apply** - Applies changes defined in a YAML file.
12. **kubectl delete** - Deletes resources defined in a YAML file.
13. **kubectl scale** - Scales a deployment to the desired number of replicas.
14. **kubectl expose** - Exposes a pod or deployment as a service.
15. **kubectl exec** - Executes a command in a running pod.
16. **kubectl port-forward** - Forwards a local port to a port in a pod.
17. **kubectl get configmaps** - Lists all ConfigMaps in the namespace.
18. **kubectl get secrets** - Lists all Secrets in the namespace.
19. **kubectl edit** - Edits a resource definition directly in the editor.
20. **kubectl rollout status** - Displays the status of a deployment rollout.

### **Advanced Kubernetes Commands**

21. **kubectl rollout undo** - Rolls back a deployment to a previous revision.
22. **kubectl top nodes** - Shows resource usage for nodes.
23. **kubectl top pods** - Displays resource usage for pods.
24. **kubectl cordon** - Marks a node as unschedulable.
25. **kubectl uncordon** - Marks a node as schedulable.
26. **kubectl drain** - Safely evicts all pods from a node.
27. **kubectl taint** - Adds a taint to a node to control pod placement.
28. **kubectl get events** - Lists all events in the cluster.
29. **kubectl apply -k** - Applies resources from a kustomization directory.
30. **kubectl config view** - Displays the kubeconfig file.
31. **kubectl config use-context** - Switches the active context in kubeconfig.
32. **kubectl debug** - Creates a debugging session for a pod.
33. **kubectl delete namespace** - Deletes a namespace and its resources.
34. **kubectl patch** - Updates a resource using a patch.
35. **kubectl rollout history** - Shows the rollout history of a deployment.
36. **kubectl autoscale** - Automatically scales a deployment based on resource usage.
37. **kubectl label** - Adds or modifies a label on a resource.
38. **kubectl annotate** - Adds or modifies an annotation on a resource.
39. **kubectl delete pv** - Deletes a PersistentVolume (PV).
40. **kubectl get ingress** - Lists all Ingress resources in the namespace.
41. **kubectl create configmap** - Creates a ConfigMap from a file or literal values.
42. **kubectl create secret** - Creates a Secret from a file or literal values.
43. **kubectl api-resources** - Lists all available API resources in the cluster.
44. **kubectl api-versions** - Lists all API versions supported by the cluster.
45. **kubectl get crds** - Lists all CustomResourceDefinitions (CRDs).

### **Basic Helm Commands**

1.  **helm help** - Displays help for the Helm CLI or a specific command.
2.  **helm version** - Shows the Helm client and server version.
3.  **helm repo add** - Adds a new chart repository.
4.  **helm repo update** - Updates all Helm chart repositories to the latest version.
5.  **helm repo list** - Lists all the repositories added to Helm.
6.  **helm search hub** - Searches for charts on Helm Hub.
7.  **helm search repo** - Searches for charts in the repositories.
8.  **helm show chart** - Displays information about a chart, including metadata and dependencies.
9.  **helm install** - Installs a chart into a Kubernetes cluster.
10. **helm upgrade** - Upgrades an existing release with a new version of the chart.
11. **helm upgrade --install** - Installs a chart if it isn’t installed or upgrades it if it exists.
12. **helm uninstall** - Uninstalls a release.
13. **helm list** - Lists all the releases installed on the Kubernetes cluster.
14. **helm status** - Displays the status of a release.
15. **helm create** - Creates a new Helm chart in a specified directory.
16. **helm lint** - Lints a chart to check for common errors.
17. **helm package** - Packages a chart into a .tgz file.
18. **helm template** - Renders the Kubernetes YAML files from a chart without installing it.
19. **helm dependency update** - Updates the dependencies in the Chart.yaml file.

### **Advanced Helm Commands**

20. **helm rollback** - Rolls back a release to a previous version.
21. **helm history** - Displays the history of a release.
22. **helm push** - Pushes a chart to a Helm chart repository.
23. **helm list -n** - Lists releases in a specific Kubernetes namespace.
24. **helm install --kube-context** - Installs a chart to a Kubernetes cluster in a specific kubeconfig context.
25. **helm upgrade --kube-context** - Upgrades a release in a specific Kubernetes context.
26. **helm dependency build** - Builds dependencies for a Helm chart.
27. **helm dependency list** - Lists all dependencies for a chart.
28. **helm rollback --recreate-pods** - Rolls back to a previous version and recreates pods.
29. **helm history --max** - Limits the number of versions shown in the release history.

### **Basic Terraform Commands**

50. **terraform --help** - Displays general help for Terraform CLI commands.
51. **terraform init** - Initializes the working directory and downloads provider plugins.
52. **terraform validate** - Validates the Terraform configuration files for syntax errors.
53. **terraform plan** - Creates an execution plan showing what changes will be made.
54. **terraform apply** - Applies the changes to reach the desired infrastructure state.
55. **terraform show** - Displays the Terraform state or a plan in human-readable format.
56. **terraform output** - Displays the output values defined in the configuration.
57. **terraform destroy** - Destroys the infrastructure defined in the configuration.
58. **terraform refresh** - Updates the state file with the real infrastructure’s current state.
59. **terraform taint** - Marks a resource for recreation on the next apply.
60. **terraform untaint** - Removes the tainted status from a resource.
61. **terraform state** - Manages Terraform state files.
62. **terraform import** - Imports existing infrastructure into Terraform management.
63. **terraform graph** - Generates a graphical representation of resources and relationships.
64. **terraform providers** - Lists the providers available for the current configuration.
65. **terraform state list** - Lists all resources tracked in the Terraform state file.
66. **terraform backend** - Configures the backend for storing Terraform state remotely.
